File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("fbbcf4d8f98168f78f5c0c47a9ae56d59ec8ac84a7c9ca6b797fedfb8d62d2bd", "b7ca95d1b23c8e67416a25cedf741de0917c2096bbc9d24649eea7853d054903", "c8fd47d36bdf7c825378593ab82ed8c24d1dc52e26b507812393e24e1d5201fd") or SHA1 in~ ("fbbcf4d8f98168f78f5c0c47a9ae56d59ec8ac84a7c9ca6b797fedfb8d62d2bd", "b7ca95d1b23c8e67416a25cedf741de0917c2096bbc9d24649eea7853d054903", "c8fd47d36bdf7c825378593ab82ed8c24d1dc52e26b507812393e24e1d5201fd") or MD5 in~ ("fbbcf4d8f98168f78f5c0c47a9ae56d59ec8ac84a7c9ca6b797fedfb8d62d2bd", "b7ca95d1b23c8e67416a25cedf741de0917c2096bbc9d24649eea7853d054903", "c8fd47d36bdf7c825378593ab82ed8c24d1dc52e26b507812393e24e1d5201fd")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("fbbcf4d8f98168f78f5c0c47a9ae56d59ec8ac84a7c9ca6b797fedfb8d62d2bd", "b7ca95d1b23c8e67416a25cedf741de0917c2096bbc9d24649eea7853d054903", "c8fd47d36bdf7c825378593ab82ed8c24d1dc52e26b507812393e24e1d5201fd") or SHA1 in~ ("fbbcf4d8f98168f78f5c0c47a9ae56d59ec8ac84a7c9ca6b797fedfb8d62d2bd", "b7ca95d1b23c8e67416a25cedf741de0917c2096bbc9d24649eea7853d054903", "c8fd47d36bdf7c825378593ab82ed8c24d1dc52e26b507812393e24e1d5201fd") or MD5 in~ ("fbbcf4d8f98168f78f5c0c47a9ae56d59ec8ac84a7c9ca6b797fedfb8d62d2bd", "b7ca95d1b23c8e67416a25cedf741de0917c2096bbc9d24649eea7853d054903", "c8fd47d36bdf7c825378593ab82ed8c24d1dc52e26b507812393e24e1d5201fd")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["fbbcf4d8f98168f78f5c0c47a9ae56d59ec8ac84a7c9ca6b797fedfb8d62d2bd", "b7ca95d1b23c8e67416a25cedf741de0917c2096bbc9d24649eea7853d054903", "c8fd47d36bdf7c825378593ab82ed8c24d1dc52e26b507812393e24e1d5201fd"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Compromised jscrambler 8.14.0 npm Release Drops Rust Infostealer During Install

The Hacker News
2026-07-11 15 use cases 5 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

The jscrambler npm package was compromised, and simply installing its&nbsp;8.14.0&nbsp;release runs an infostealer on your machine. Published on July 11, 2026, the malicious version carries a&nbsp;preinstall&nbsp;hook that drops and executes a native binary, one build each for Windows, macOS, and Linux. Socket flagged the release&nbsp;six minutes after it was published. If you or one of your

Compromised jscrambler npm CLI (8.14.0 + trojanized siblings) install attempt AIDelivery High
Hunts npm/yarn/pnpm/node invocations that acquire the trojanized jscrambler CLI versions (8.14.0 and the later 8.16.0/8.17.0/8.18.0/8.20.0 confirmed by StepSecurity/SafeDep). Any acquisition of these pinned versions during the vulnerable window means the preinstall/require-time stealer may have run with the build process's access. Rationale: Anchored on the exact malicious version strings named in the article (8.14.0) plus the sibling versions confirmed by StepSecurity/SafeDep second sources; package-manager parent + pinned bad version is a near-zero-FP acquisition signal. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/jscrambler-npm-package-publishes-malicious-preinstall-binary • https://socket.dev/blog/jscrambler-supply-chain-attack • https://safedep.io/jscrambler-npm-supply-chain-compromise/
ATT&CKT1195.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher intentionally pulling 8.14.0 in an isolated sandbox to analyze
  • Internal mirror/proxy pre-fetch job caching all published jscrambler versions
  • A developer pinned to a legitimately-numbered future version that reuses one of these strings in an unrelated context
  • CI matrix job testing multiple jscrambler versions for a compatibility report
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("--dry-run","npm view","npm info"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the version string in cmdline is a real acquisition (npm ci/install) and identify the host/CI runner and the account context the install ran under. 2) Contain: isolate the host or freeze the CI runner, remove jscrambler@8.14.0/8.16.0/8.17.0/8.18.0/8.20.0 from lockfiles and package caches, and pin to 8.13.0 or 8.15.0. 3) Hunt: on any host that ran it, pivot to temp-dir hidden-binary execution (UC3), C2 IPs 37.27.122.124/57.128.246.79 (UC4), and treat all reachable cloud/npm/GitHub/AI-tool secrets as stolen and rotate.

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc

jscrambler stealer IOC file-hash / payload hash match (setup.js, intro.js, native binaries) AIInstallation Medium
Matches the exact SHA-256 hashes of the malicious loader (dist/setup.js), the binary container (dist/intro.js), and the decompressed Windows/macOS/Linux Rust payloads. High-confidence confirmation that the tampered package landed and/or the stealer executed on the host. Rationale: Uses the five verbatim SHA-256 IOCs published in the article and confirmed by StepSecurity/Socket; hash matches are definitionally article-specific and near-zero FP. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/jscrambler-npm-package-publishes-malicious-preinstall-binary • https://socket.dev/blog/jscrambler-supply-chain-attack

Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Malware-analysis sandbox intentionally holding the sample for reversing
  • AV/EDR quarantine copy retaining the file (verify FolderPath is a quarantine store before escalating)
  • Threat-intel enrichment tooling caching the payload as a reference sample
Suppression filter

Response runbook

npm/node preinstall drops & detaches hidden Rust binary from temp directory AIExploitation High
Hunts the setup.js loader behaviour: a node process (running jscrambler/dist/setup.js) that writes a randomly-named hidden file to the system temp directory and launches it detached. Targets the parent→child chain rather than a fixed binary name, since the payload uses a random '.{random}(.exe)' name. Rationale: Encodes the article's exact loader behaviour (node → jscrambler/dist/setup.js → hidden '.{random}(.exe)' in temp, launched detached); anchoring on the setup.js parent cmdline plus the hidden-in-temp naming pattern keeps it specific without a fixed payload name. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/jscrambler-npm-package-publishes-malicious-preinstall-binary • https://socket.dev/blog/jscrambler-supply-chain-attack
ATT&CKT1059.001 T1105 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate node CLI tools that unpack helper binaries into temp (e.g. some prebuilt-binary installers)
  • Electron apps extracting bundled resources with leading-dot names into temp
  • npm postinstall of a benign native-addon package writing to temp
  • Build tooling that legitimately shells out to node with a setup.js in an unrelated package
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any (@"\nvm\", @"\Program Files\nodejs\") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("electron","node-gyp") and not InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "jscrambler")
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the child binary's full path/hash and confirm the parent is jscrambler's setup.js (or node writing a hidden temp binary); hash the dropped file against UC2 IOCs. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the detached process, and preserve the temp binary for analysis before deletion. 3) Hunt: check the same host for outbound C2 (UC4), scheduled-task/LaunchAgent persistence (UC5), and secret-store reads (UC6); scope other CI runners that pulled jscrambler in the window.

        and FileName startswith ".")

| order by Timestamp desc

Hidden temp-dropped binary establishes Windows scheduled-task persistence (1-minute relaunch) AIInstallation Medium
Hunts the stealer's Windows persistence: a hidden scheduled task set to relaunch the payload every minute, created by a binary running from the temp directory. Correlates task creation with a hidden/temp initiating process, or matches the tell-tale hidden + PT1M repetition in the task definition. Rationale: Encodes two article-specific persistence traits (hidden task + relaunch every minute) and ties task creation to a hidden/temp initiating process, which is the stealer's exact behaviour; kept at hunting tier because API-registered tasks may not always surface a temp parent. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/jscrambler-npm-package-publishes-malicious-preinstall-binary

Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate agents that create 1-minute polling tasks (some monitoring/telemetry tools)
  • Software updaters that register frequent-recurrence tasks
  • Admin-authored maintenance task with a hidden flag
  • Portable app run from temp that self-registers a keepalive task
Suppression filter

Temp-dropped stealer fans out across credential, wallet, browser and AI-tool config stores AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects a single hidden/temp-resident process reading three or more distinct high-value secret stores the jscrambler Rust stealer targets: cloud creds (AWS/Azure/GCP), crypto wallets (MetaMask/Phantom/Exodus), Bitwarden, browser cookies/passwords, and — the article's novel target — AI-coding-tool configs (Claude Desktop, Cursor, Windsurf, VS Code, Zed) holding API keys and MCP server credentials. Rationale: Directly enumerates the article's stated stealer target list — including its novel AI-coding-tool/MCP credential targets — and requires a hidden/temp process (the dropped payload) to touch ≥3 distinct stores, converting a broad target list into a staged-theft fan-out signal. Cross-checked against: • https://socket.dev/blog/jscrambler-supply-chain-attack • https://safedep.io/jscrambler-npm-supply-chain-compromise/
ATT&CKT1005 T1555.003 T1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate backup/sync agent snapshotting user profile directories
  • Endpoint DLP or secret-scanner tooling that reads credential stores by design
  • A developer-run migration/dotfiles tool that touches ~/.ssh, .aws and browser profiles
  • Antivirus full-disk scan reading these paths (verify initiating process is not the AV engine)
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName startswith "." or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any (@"\AppData\Local\Temp\", "/tmp/", "/var/folders/")
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the initiating process and confirm it is a hidden/temp binary (not a sanctioned backup/DLP agent); list the exact stores it read. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the process, and immediately begin secret rotation for every store touched (cloud keys, Bitwarden vault, browser sessions, AI-tool/MCP API keys, SSH keys) and move any crypto off the host. 3) Hunt: pivot to C2 egress (UC4) to confirm exfiltration and check other CI runners for the same fan-out pattern.

ATT&CKT1078.004 T1550.001
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • An analyst intentionally testing the blocked IPs from a corporate identity for validation
  • IP later reassigned by the hosting provider (Hetzner/OVH) to a benign service a user legitimately signs in from
  • A VPN/proxy exit node coincidentally sharing the address
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the account and the API calls/apps accessed from the C2 IP; determine whether the account's keys were reachable by a host that ran jscrambler. 2) Contain: revoke/rotate the affected access keys and tokens, force re-auth, and add a Conditional Access / SCP block on the two IPs. 3) Hunt: enumerate all actions taken by the compromised principal (new IAM users/keys, role assumptions, resource changes), and correlate with endpoint UC1–UC6 hits to find the originally-infected build machine.

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest

| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine

// article (matching shape with the Defender query).

| where Account !endswith "$"

   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("a742de963f14a92d24ebcbc7b44ac867e23a20d31d1b0094a13a4f83287f4e60", "a41a523ef9517aab37ed6eea0ec881821bdcb7aefcb5c5f603adc7907f868c86", "fbbcf4d8f98168f78f5c0c47a9ae56d59ec8ac84a7c9ca6b797fedfb8d62d2bd", "b7ca95d1b23c8e67416a25cedf741de0917c2096bbc9d24649eea7853d054903", "c8fd47d36bdf7c825378593ab82ed8c24d1dc52e26b507812393e24e1d5201fd") or SHA1 in~ ("a742de963f14a92d24ebcbc7b44ac867e23a20d31d1b0094a13a4f83287f4e60", "a41a523ef9517aab37ed6eea0ec881821bdcb7aefcb5c5f603adc7907f868c86", "fbbcf4d8f98168f78f5c0c47a9ae56d59ec8ac84a7c9ca6b797fedfb8d62d2bd", "b7ca95d1b23c8e67416a25cedf741de0917c2096bbc9d24649eea7853d054903", "c8fd47d36bdf7c825378593ab82ed8c24d1dc52e26b507812393e24e1d5201fd") or MD5 in~ ("a742de963f14a92d24ebcbc7b44ac867e23a20d31d1b0094a13a4f83287f4e60", "a41a523ef9517aab37ed6eea0ec881821bdcb7aefcb5c5f603adc7907f868c86", "fbbcf4d8f98168f78f5c0c47a9ae56d59ec8ac84a7c9ca6b797fedfb8d62d2bd", "b7ca95d1b23c8e67416a25cedf741de0917c2096bbc9d24649eea7853d054903", "c8fd47d36bdf7c825378593ab82ed8c24d1dc52e26b507812393e24e1d5201fd")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("a742de963f14a92d24ebcbc7b44ac867e23a20d31d1b0094a13a4f83287f4e60", "a41a523ef9517aab37ed6eea0ec881821bdcb7aefcb5c5f603adc7907f868c86", "fbbcf4d8f98168f78f5c0c47a9ae56d59ec8ac84a7c9ca6b797fedfb8d62d2bd", "b7ca95d1b23c8e67416a25cedf741de0917c2096bbc9d24649eea7853d054903", "c8fd47d36bdf7c825378593ab82ed8c24d1dc52e26b507812393e24e1d5201fd") or SHA1 in~ ("a742de963f14a92d24ebcbc7b44ac867e23a20d31d1b0094a13a4f83287f4e60", "a41a523ef9517aab37ed6eea0ec881821bdcb7aefcb5c5f603adc7907f868c86", "fbbcf4d8f98168f78f5c0c47a9ae56d59ec8ac84a7c9ca6b797fedfb8d62d2bd", "b7ca95d1b23c8e67416a25cedf741de0917c2096bbc9d24649eea7853d054903", "c8fd47d36bdf7c825378593ab82ed8c24d1dc52e26b507812393e24e1d5201fd") or MD5 in~ ("a742de963f14a92d24ebcbc7b44ac867e23a20d31d1b0094a13a4f83287f4e60", "a41a523ef9517aab37ed6eea0ec881821bdcb7aefcb5c5f603adc7907f868c86", "fbbcf4d8f98168f78f5c0c47a9ae56d59ec8ac84a7c9ca6b797fedfb8d62d2bd", "b7ca95d1b23c8e67416a25cedf741de0917c2096bbc9d24649eea7853d054903", "c8fd47d36bdf7c825378593ab82ed8c24d1dc52e26b507812393e24e1d5201fd")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["a742de963f14a92d24ebcbc7b44ac867e23a20d31d1b0094a13a4f83287f4e60", "a41a523ef9517aab37ed6eea0ec881821bdcb7aefcb5c5f603adc7907f868c86", "fbbcf4d8f98168f78f5c0c47a9ae56d59ec8ac84a7c9ca6b797fedfb8d62d2bd", "b7ca95d1b23c8e67416a25cedf741de0917c2096bbc9d24649eea7853d054903", "c8fd47d36bdf7c825378593ab82ed8c24d1dc52e26b507812393e24e1d5201fd"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Actions on Objectives
Likely
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Article-specific behavioural hunt — Hackers Weaponize Balochistan Police Portal in Multi-Group Espionage Campaigns
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] cms_plugin.exe Rust stager execution + payload pull from 193.42.25.65 · [LLM] Fake 360Safe.exe (.NET) reflectively loading AsyncRAT · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Beaconing to Balochistan Police espionage C2 / stager IP set · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
cms_plugin.exe Rust stager execution + payload pull from 193.42.25.65 AIInstallation High
Hunts the custom 'cms_plugin.exe' implant that masqueraded as a Complaint Management System portal update on Balochistan Police infrastructure. The Rust stager variant downloads a next stage from 193.42.25.65 and prints 'Update Complete! Please refresh the page'. Fires on the named binary executing or any host reaching the stager download IP. Rationale: cms_plugin.exe is a unique implant filename and 193.42.25.65 is the confirmed Rust-stager download server named in the SentinelLABS report; both are non-generic, so a match is effectively a true positive. Cross-checked SentinelOne report and The Record write-up. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sentinelone.com/labs/one-target-china-india-espionage-converge-on-pakistani-law-enforcement/ • https://therecord.media/china-india-ran-separate-spy-campaigns-against-same-police-force
ATT&CKT1105 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimately-named plugin binary in an unrelated product also called cms_plugin.exe (rare; validate hash and path)
  • Security researcher / IR analyst detonating the sample in a sandbox VM enrolled in EDR
  • Threat-intel enrichment appliance actively probing 193.42.25.65 for research
  • Proxy/NAT egress IP collision where 193.42.25.65 is re-used by a benign CDN in future (validate at trigger time)
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !startswith "C:\\Sandbox\\" and DeviceName !in~ ("IR-SANDBOX-01")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the process tree, SHA256, and folder path for cms_plugin.exe; confirm parent (likely w3wp.exe/web-app service on a CMS/web server) and whether the host is an internet-facing police web application server. 2) Contain: isolate the host, block 193.42.25.65 at the perimeter, and preserve the binary for reversing. 3) Hunt: sweep all web servers for cms_plugin.exe uploads, search web logs on cms.balochistanpolice.gov.pk-style portals for the file, and pivot on the SHA1s and the full campaign C2 IP set (see C2 UC).
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteIP == "193.42.25.65" or InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "cms_plugin.exe"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessSHA256, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl
| order by Timestamp desc
union
(SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where tolower(NewProcessName) endswith "\\cms_plugin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, Signal="process", NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName, Dest=""),
(CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where DestinationIP == "193.42.25.65"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer=SourceHostName, Account=SourceUserName, Signal="network", NewProcessName=ProcessName, CommandLine="", ParentProcessName="", Dest=DestinationIP)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Fake 360Safe.exe (.NET) reflectively loading AsyncRAT AIInstallation Medium
Hunts the second cms_plugin.exe variant: a .NET executable that masquerades as '360Safe.exe' (a legitimate Qihoo 360 Total Security binary) to reflectively load an in-memory AsyncRAT client. Detects a process named 360Safe.exe whose file metadata is NOT Qihoo/360 or which runs from outside the legitimate 360 install path. Rationale: The report states one implant variant masquerades specifically as 360Safe.exe (Qihoo 360) to reflectively load AsyncRAT; a 360Safe.exe binary running outside the genuine Qihoo install path (or lacking Qihoo file metadata) is a high-fidelity masquerade signal, especially in a Pakistani gov environment unlikely to run Chinese AV. Corroborated by SentinelOne report and Kaspersky's Mysterious Elephant AsyncRAT/Remcos tradecraft. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sentinelone.com/labs/one-target-china-india-espionage-converge-on-pakistani-law-enforcement/ • https://securelist.com/mysterious-elephant-apt-ttps-and-tools/117596/
ATT&CKT1036.005 T1620
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Genuine Qihoo 360 Total Security installed to a non-default drive/path (validate publisher signature)
  • Portable/standalone 360 Total Security run from a USB or Downloads folder by a user
  • Software-deployment tool staging the real 360Safe.exe in a temp cache before install
  • AV bake-off / procurement testing of 360 Total Security by IT
Suppression filter
| where not(ProcessVersionInfoCompanyName has_any ("360","Qihoo"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: check the digital signature and ProcessVersionInfoCompanyName of the 360Safe.exe image — genuine is signed by Qihoo/360; the implant will be unsigned or mis-signed. Inspect for .NET/CLR module loads and outbound connections. 2) Contain: isolate the host, capture memory (AsyncRAT is in-memory), block associated C2 IPs. 3) Hunt: search DeviceImageLoadEvents for clr.dll/mscoree loading into 360Safe.exe, and correlate with cms_plugin.exe drops and the campaign C2 IP set.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "360Safe.exe"
| where not(ProcessVersionInfoCompanyName has_any ("360","Qihoo")) or FolderPath !startswith "C:\\Program Files"
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessVersionInfoCompanyName, ProcessVersionInfoProductName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where tolower(NewProcessName) endswith "\\360safe.exe"
| where not(tolower(NewProcessName) startswith "c:\\program files (x86)\\360\\") and not(tolower(NewProcessName) startswith "c:\\program files\\360\\")
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName

Beaconing to Balochistan Police espionage C2 / stager IP set AICommand & Control High
Hunts outbound connections to the confirmed command-and-control and staging IPs across the four converging clusters (Cobalt Strike C2 142.171.183.8, Rust-stager host 193.42.25.65, plus the campaign infrastructure block) targeting Pakistani law enforcement. Any internal host reaching these IPs warrants investigation. Rationale: 142.171.183.8 is the named Cobalt Strike C2 and 193.42.25.65 the stager host; the remaining IPs are the pre-extracted campaign infrastructure. These are static, second-source-consistent IOCs, so egress to any of them is high-fidelity for this specific converging-actor campaign. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sentinelone.com/labs/one-target-china-india-espionage-converge-on-pakistani-law-enforcement/ • https://therecord.media/china-india-ran-separate-spy-campaigns-against-same-police-force
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1105
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • An IOC IP later reassigned to a shared hosting/CDN provider serving benign sites (re-validate WHOIS at trigger time)
  • Internal threat-intel platform or vulnerability scanner actively probing the C2 IPs
  • Security researcher pivoting on the infrastructure from a corporate egress IP
  • Firewall/proxy that logs a blocked (denied) attempt — confirm DeviceAction/outcome is allow before escalating
Suppression filter
DeviceNetworkEvents | where ActionType in ("ConnectionSuccess","ConnectionAttempt") and RemoteIPType == "Public"
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the source host and the InitiatingProcess making the connection; check whether the connection succeeded vs was blocked, and beacon regularity (jitter/interval). 2) Contain: block all listed IPs at the perimeter, isolate any host with a successful/repeated connection, and snapshot memory for the process (PlugX/ShadowPad/Cobalt Strike/AsyncRAT are memory-resident). 3) Hunt: pivot on the initiating process hash against the campaign SHA1 list, sweep for cms_plugin.exe and fake 360Safe.exe, and review the FortiMail email gateway and web-app servers for the initial foothold.

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )

          FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine,

Critical Zimbra Flaw Could Let Crafted Emails Run Malicious Code in User Sessions

The Hacker News
2026-07-11 8 use cases 3 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

Zimbra is urging customers to apply updates to address a critical security vulnerability impacting the Classic Web Client that could result in arbitrary code execution. The vulnerability has been described as a case of stored cross-site scripting (XSS) that could allow specially crafted emails to execute malicious scripts in a user's session. It has yet to be assigned a CVE identifier. "The

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Zimbra Collaboration Classic Web Client below patched build 10.1.19 (stored XSS exposure)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → Suspicious browser extension installation
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs · Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
Zimbra Collaboration Classic Web Client below patched build 10.1.19 (stored XSS exposure) AIExploitation Medium
Surfaces managed hosts running Zimbra Collaboration Suite older than 10.1.19, the build that fixes the July 2026 Classic Web Client stored-XSS flaw where a crafted email runs JavaScript in the victim's mailbox session. This is an exposure/attack-surface hunt: no in-the-wild payload was published, so the highest-fidelity pivot is the exact patched version and Zimbra software-inventory signal. Rationale: Uses the two concrete strings the article/advisory actually give: vendor+product 'Zimbra Collaboration' and the exact fixed build '10.1.19'. The new flaw has no CVE yet, so a version-threshold inventory hunt is the only non-generic pivot; the Sentinel arm also folds in the three prior weaponised Zimbra XSS CVEs the article names (CVE-2025-27915, CVE-2024-27443, CVE-2023-37580) that TVM already tracks. Cross-checked against: • https://cyberpress.org/zimbra-10-1-19-fixes-stored-xss-flaw/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/zimbra-urges-customers-to-patch-critical-web-client-xss-flaw/ • https://securityaffairs.com/195130/hacking/update-now-critical-zimbra-classic-web-client-flaw-could-expose-mailboxes.html
ATT&CKT1203 T1189
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Host mid-upgrade to 10.1.19 where inventory snapshot still shows the old 10.1.18 build until the next scan
  • Zimbra servers where only the Modern Web Client is enabled (article states the flaw is Classic-Web-Client-only, so these are technically not exploitable)
  • Dev/test or decommissioned Zimbra appliances retained deliberately on old builds
  • Third-party product bundling a 'Zimbra' string in its software name (e.g. a Zimbra connector/plugin) inflating the match
  • Air-gapped/internet-isolated Zimbra hosts where the crafted-email vector is not reachable
Suppression filter
| where EndOfSupportStatus != "Upgrade required" or isnotempty(SoftwareVersion)   // keep only hosts with a resolvable installed build; drop empty-version inventory stubs
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the flagged host actually serves the Classic Web Client (check whether zimbra-mbox-webclient-war is deployed and Classic UI is enabled) and that the version is genuinely <10.1.19 rather than an in-flight upgrade. 2) Contain: prioritise upgrade to ZCS 10.1.19 ('Daffodil'); if immediate patching is impossible, disable the Classic Web Client and force users to the Modern client, and (for hosts migrated from 10.0.x/9.0.x/8.8.15) reapply the SNMP mitigation. 3) Hunt: review Zimbra mailbox audit/access logs on the exposed host for anomalous session activity around message render — new auto-forward/filter rules, mass reads, contact/data exports, or session-token reuse from unusual IPs — since successful XSS yields mailbox, session-data and account-setting access.
DeviceTvmSoftwareInventory
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where SoftwareVendor has "zimbra" or SoftwareName has "zimbra"
| where SoftwareName has_any ("collaboration","zimbra","zcs","mbox-webclient")
| extend InstalledVer = parse_version(SoftwareVersion)
| where InstalledVer < parse_version("10.1.19")   // 10.1.19 = build that fixes the Classic Web Client stored XSS (7 Jul 2026)
| project DeviceName, DeviceId, OSPlatform, OSVersion, SoftwareVendor, SoftwareName, SoftwareVersion, EndOfSupportStatus
| sort by SoftwareVersion asc
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where SoftwareVendor has "zimbra" or SoftwareName has "zimbra"
| extend InstalledVer = parse_version(SoftwareVersion)
| where InstalledVer < parse_version("10.1.19")   // pre-fix Classic Web Client build
   or CveId in ("CVE-2025-27915","CVE-2024-27443","CVE-2023-37580")   // prior weaponised Zimbra XSS the article cites
| summarize KnownCVEs = make_set(CveId), MaxSeverity = max(VulnerabilitySeverityLevel) by DeviceName, DeviceId, SoftwareVendor, SoftwareName, SoftwareVersion
| sort by SoftwareVersion asc

title: Browser Extension Installation via Registry
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0a
status: experimental
description: |
    Registry writes under the browser extension policy keys —
    administrative install of an extension. Often used by adware /
    info-stealers to bypass user-level extension controls.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1176/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1176
    - attack.persistence

    category: registry_event
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        TargetObject|contains:
            - '\Software\Google\Chrome\Extensions\'
            - '\Software\Microsoft\Edge\Extensions\'
            - '\Software\Mozilla\Firefox\Extensions\'
    condition: selection
falsepositives:
    - Group-policy-managed extension whitelists
level: medium

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1555/003/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1539
    - attack.t1555.003
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'
            - '\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\'
    selection_file:
        TargetFilename|endswith:
            - '\Login Data'
            - '\Cookies'
            - '\logins.json'
            - '\cookies.sqlite'
    filter_browser:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\chrome.exe'
            - '\msedge.exe'
            - '\firefox.exe'
            - '\brave.exe'
            - '\opera.exe'
    condition: selection_path and selection_file and not filter_browser
falsepositives:
    - Backup utilities reading user profile files
    - Browser sync agents from non-stock vendors
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\logins.json*"

Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-27915", "CVE-2023-37580", "CVE-2024-27443")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-27915", "CVE-2023-37580", "CVE-2024-27443")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: LSASS Process Access Outside Microsoft-Signed Tooling
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d04
status: experimental
description: |
    Anything opening lsass.exe with read-memory access that isn't a
    Microsoft signed component (Defender, csrss, etc.) is suspect.
    Mimikatz / Pypykatz / nanodump signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02

    - attack.credential-access

    category: process_access
    product: windows

    selection:
        TargetImage|endswith: '\lsass.exe'
    filter_known_good:
        SourceImage|endswith:
            - '\MsSense.exe'
            - '\MsMpEng.exe'
            - '\csrss.exe'
            - '\svchost.exe'
            - '\wininit.exe'
            - '\services.exe'
            - '\lsm.exe'
            - '\SearchProtocolHost.exe'
    condition: selection and not filter_known_good
falsepositives:
    - Third-party EDR / forensic tools — extend the filter list
level: high

Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement Actions on Objectives High
psexec / paexec / smbexec / wmic /node — service install over SMB from remote host.
ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
   or (FileName =~ "wmic.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "/node:")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688            // process-create (audit-policy required)
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: PsExec / SMB Lateral Movement Tooling Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d03
status: experimental
description: |
    PsExec, paexec, smbexec, csexec, remcom — admin-grade tooling that
    pivots over SMB. Pair with `wmic /node:` for the full cluster.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1021/002/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1569/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1021.002
    - attack.t1569.002
    - attack.lateral-movement
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_psexec:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\psexec.exe'
            - '\psexec64.exe'
            - '\psexesvc.exe'
            - '\paexec.exe'
            - '\csexec.exe'
            - '\remcom.exe'
            - '\smbexec.py'
    selection_wmic_remote:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\wmic.exe'
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '/node:'
    condition: selection_psexec or selection_wmic_remote
falsepositives:
    - Sysadmins running approved remote-management tooling — wrap with
      a known-admin-host watchlist
level: high

       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

Network connections to article IPs / domains Command & Control High
Outbound traffic to attacker infrastructure named in the article.
ATT&CKT1071

DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where RemoteIP in ("") or RemoteUrl has_any ("attacker.example")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
let BadIPs     = dynamic([""]);
let BadDomains = dynamic(["attacker.example"]);
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DstIpAddr in (BadIPs)
   or Url has_any (BadDomains)
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, Url, NetworkProtocol
| order by TimeGenerated desc

    article-specific destinations. For dynamic IOC management, use a
